\chapter*{Background}\label{background}
\addcontentsline{toc}{chapter}{Background}

\markboth{Background}{Background}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, colframe=quarto-callout-warning-color-frame, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-warning-color}{\faExclamationTriangle}\hspace{0.5em}{Note}, left=2mm, coltitle=black, rightrule=.15mm, opacityback=0, leftrule=.75mm, breakable, toprule=.15mm, colback=white, bottomrule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm, opacitybacktitle=0.6, arc=.35mm, titlerule=0mm, bottomtitle=1mm, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-warning-color!10!white]

\textbf{This is a live, working document - it will be updated
periodically as we obtain more data. The most current date in the data
may vary between output type as we do not receive all the relevant data
at once e.g., we do not receive movement data from the AIM system for
last year until the later half of the current year and as a result there
may be a delay in producing some of the output such as estimating the
\emph{M.bovis} lesion rate within healthly slaughtered animals (which
relies on knowing the number of animals sent to slaughter from AIM).
This also means that we don't have last years herd-type or herd-size AIM
related metrics. In the case of herd-type which is pivotal to much of
the analysis, we carry the latest estimate forward until the new data
comes in.}

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

This is an accompanying website to supplement a paper submitted to
journal XXX. The purpose of this website is to have a near-real-time
version of the output from the paper using more recent data as we obtain
it. It also acts as a location to include excess output that didn't make
it into the main paper but which we think is still useful for readers.
Additional analysis will be added over time.

INCLUDE paper reference here

The paper highlights various trends from different components of the
bovine tuberculosis (bTB) eradication programme in Ireland (from 2008
onwards). This includes cattle population demographics, herd enterprise
types, bTB case numbers, herd-level bTB breakdowns, lesion detection at
slaughter inspections both in bTB positive animals and routinely
slaughtered animals. The data used has been obtained from the Irish
national bTB eradication programme (Animal Health Computer System (AHCS)
(Department of Agriculture, Food and the Marine (DAFM)), Animal
Identification and Movement System (AIM), Laboratory Information
Management System (LIMS)), which included results from herd-level skin
tests, animal-level laboratory tests (from animals with suspect bTB
lesions) and the animal-level IFN-γ blood tests. A bTB eradication
timeline is also included which highlights some of the major trials and
policy and diagnostic changes introduced during the programme.

The data has been processed by the Centre for Veterinary Epidemiology
and Risk Analysis \href{https://www.ucd.ie/cvera/}{CVERA} for research
purposes and as a result, there may be minor discrepancies between DAFM
and our output. For official documentation from the bTB eradication
programme visit
\href{https://www.gov.ie/en/department-of-agriculture-food-and-the-marine/collections/bovine-tb/}{DAFM}.

\section*{What's included?}\label{whats-included}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What's included?}

\markright{What's included?}

The wesbite is divided into these parts:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \hyperref[sec-timeline]{\textbf{Interactive timeline}}: Highlights
  major trials, diagnostic and policy changes during the Irish bTB
  eradication (2005 - 2023).
\item
  \hyperref[sec-demographics]{\textbf{Demographics}}: Changes to
  demographics over time (herd-types, bovine numbers etc.).
\item
  \hyperref[sec-casenumbers]{\textbf{bTB case numbers and
  herd-prevalence}}: Examines bTB case numbers over time along with herd
  prevalence by different herd-types.
\item
  \textbf{Lesion prevalence}: This includes both visible lesions in
  animals already classified as a \hyperref[sec-btblesions]{bTB case
  entering the factory} and \emph{M.bovis} confirmed
  \hyperref[sec-healthlylesion]{lesions found in animals at routine
  slaughter} (healthy animals)
\item
  \hyperref[sec-breakdown]{\textbf{bTB breakdowns (BDs)}}: This explores
  changes to herd-level BDs over time.
\item
  \hyperref[sec-timeseries]{\textbf{Time-series and forecasting}}: This
  includes time-series analysis and basic forecasting of the count of
  bTB cases.
\item
  \hyperref[sec-testtypes]{\textbf{SICTT test-types}}: This explores the
  different skin test-types over time.
\item
  \hyperref[sec-publications]{\textbf{CVERA related bTB publications}}:
  Almost an exhaustive list of bTB related publications from CVERA (from
  1990 to current day). This encompasses publications that may not be
  directly from CVERA (e.g., where CVERA member is only a co-author on
  the publication, the list also includes publications from the
  \href{https://www.ucd.ie/vetmed/research/researchthemes/veterinaryepidemiologyandnationaldiseasecontrol/}{Tuberculosis
  Diagnostics \& Immunology Research Laboratory} in UCD).
\end{itemize}

\section*{What's not included (yet)?}\label{whats-not-included-yet}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{What's not included (yet)?}

\markright{What's not included (yet)?}

The focus is primarily at a national level although some county level
output is included. We acknowledge there is a strong spatio-temporal
distribution of bTB in Ireland but exploring spatial trends is not the
focus of this particular work. For a recent spatial analysis of bTB
trends in Ireland, see
\href{https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S187758452100040X}{Madden
et al.} and for a more current spatial exploration of bTB, see
\href{https://www.arcgis.com/apps/dashboards/9306f7eb60b1401a8b18f76f61f20aea}{here}.
There is also no analysis included on age-specific bTB trends - that is
a separate piece of work that is ongoing (but may be added here over
time).

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\part{Demographics}

\chapter{Demographics (herd-types, herd size, bovine
population)}\label{sec-demographics}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\(~\) \(~\)

\section{Data and definitions}\label{data-and-definitions}

\subsection{Study period}\label{study-period}

Data is available for 2005-2024 but we found many instances in the early
years where a herd was in a BD (\texttt{trading\_status\ ==\ withdrawn})
but no bTB cases were reported (the BD may have begun before our
earliest date) and so to make fair comparisons between years we have
only included data from 2008 onwards.

\subsection{Herd type categorisations}\label{herd-type-categorisations}

We have access to three herd type categorisations:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  one assigned to a herd on AHCS:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Dairy
  \item
    Beef
  \item
    Suckler
  \item
    Other
  \end{itemize}
\item
  \href{https://www.nature.com/articles/s41598-021-82373-3}{Brock et
  al.~(2021)} created two model based herd categorisation.
  \href{https://irishvetjournal.biomedcentral.com/articles/10.1186/s13620-022-00212-x}{Brock
  et al.~(2022)} used these to explore enterprises from 2015 to 2019,
  here we expand on this work and additionally explore more years of
  data. The original categorisation included \texttt{Seasonal}
  herd-types but these are now no longer included. They have moved into
  \texttt{Traders} or \texttt{Fatteners} respectively, based on their
  profiles.

  The main Brock et al.~herd types are:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Dairy
  \item
    Beef
  \item
    Store
  \item
    Fattener
  \item
    Mixed
  \item
    Trader
  \item
    Unknown
  \end{itemize}

  \(~\)

  and the Brock et al.~sub-classes are:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Dairy
  \item
    Dairy no rearing - contract
  \item
    Dairy non rearing - no contract
  \item
    Dairy rearing females
  \item
    Dairy rearing male calves
  \item
    Beef pedigree
  \item
    Beef suckling to beef
  \item
    Beef suckling to weanlings
  \item
    Beef suckling to youngstock
  \item
    Beef suckling to youngstock - no rearing
  \item
    Store beef females
  \item
    Store beef males
  \item
    Store beef mixed
  \item
    Store dairy males
  \item
    Fattener
  \item
    Mixed
  \item
    Trader
  \item
    Unknown
  \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

The following is a brief summary of the main Brock et al.~herd-types:

\textbf{Dairy}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Residency in the herd is longer than 30 days for at least 50\% of the
  herd.
\item
  Proportion of animals in a herd that have ever calved by September,
  relative to herd size is greater than 25\%.
\item
  66\% or more of the animals are of a dairy breed.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Beef}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  (generally just suckler from AHCS I think)
\item
  Residency in the herd is longer than 30 days for at least 50\% of the
  herd
\item
  Proportion of animals in a herd that have ever calved by September,
  relative to herd size is greater than 25\%.
\item
  Less than 33\% of the animals are of a dairy breed.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Mixed}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Residency in the herd is longer than 30 days for at least 50\% of the
  herd.
\item
  Proportion of animals in a herd that have ever calved by September,
  relative to herd size is greater than 25\%.
\item
  33\% or more of the animals and less than 66\% are of a dairy breed.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Store}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Residency in the herd is longer than 30 days for at least 50\% of the
  herd.
\item
  Less than 25\% of the animals in the herd have calved in the past
\item
  Less than 50\% of the animals in the herd go directly to slaughter
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Fattener}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Residency in the herd is longer than 30 days for at least 50\% of the
  herd.
\item
  Proportion of animals in a herd that have ever calved by September,
  relative to herd size is greater than 25\%.
\item
  50\% or more of the animals in the herd go directly to slaughter.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Trader}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  50\% or more of the animals spend less than 30 days in the herd.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Seasonal}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Animals are kept in the herd for only part of the year. (As of 2024,
  ``seasonal'' is no longer a category - they have been moved into
  ``trader'' and ``fattener'' categories respectively)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Unknown} - Self-explanatory.

\subsection{Newly established herds - AHCS herd
types}\label{newly-established-herds---ahcs-herd-types}

To explore any potential impact of the removal of milk quotas (2015),
the distibution of newly established herds were plotted (particularly to
see if there was an increase in dairy herds). Here, newly established
herds were herds that did not have a SICTT before 2015 (but had at least
one after 2015). This may be slightly different to an official list
provided by DAFM but it should be a very close approximation (I think!).

\part{Lesion prevalence}

\chapter{Prevalence of visible lesions seperately by SICTT and IFN-γ
positive animals at slaughter}\label{sec-btblesions}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\(~\) \(~\)

\newpage

\section{Introduction}\label{introduction}

This document presents the prevalence of visible lesions at slaughter
within bTB positive animals separately by those classified by the SICTT
and IFN-γ. There was no facility within AHCS prior to May 2019 to record
IFN-γ positive animals. Before this, if an animal had a positive IFN-γ
result, their SICTT results were reinterpreted as positive. As a result,
we have had to use old raw IFN-γ data to try and differentiate between
which animals were positive on IFN-γ or SICTT. The old data has some
missing tag numbers etc. and so we cannot be certain that we have
assigned all cases to either IFN-γ or SICTT correctly. However, we
believe, the vast majority of the data is correct.

This is the difference between overall lesion prevalence document and
this one (i.e.~we make an attempt to seperate SICTT and IFN-γ positive
trends).

\section{Definitions}\label{definitions}

\subsection{Proportion of animals with lesions at routine
slaughter}\label{proportion-of-animals-with-lesions-at-routine-slaughter}

If an animal is classified as bTB positive before entering an abattoir
or a meatplant, it must have a permit to enter. Any animal with a permit
will get a visual inspection for lesions. In rare instances, a bTB
positive animal may be sent to a knackery instead of an abattoir or
meatplant (e.g.~if the animal dies before being sent to the factory). In
these cases, a permit may not be issued and a visual inspection does not
routinely take place in a knackery. For these reasons, only animals with
a permit (and thus, a visual check completed) are considered for this
document.

Of all SICTT and IFN-γ positive animals, this is the breakdown of the
number of animals with a permit:

\begin{verbatim}
 per_type      n      percent valid_percent
     ER26 304995 0.8371738743  0.8546604981
    ER26A   2293 0.0062940038  0.0064254710
    ER26B    285 0.0007822901  0.0007986303
    ER26D    195 0.0005352511  0.0005464312
  ER26GIF  38032 0.1043931762  0.1065737080
    ER26R    281 0.0007713105  0.0007874214
    ER26S  10002 0.0274542635  0.0280277195
 ER26SGIF    778 0.0021355146  0.0021801206
     <NA>   7454 0.0204603159            NA
\end{verbatim}

Of importance here is that \texttt{ER26GIF} and \texttt{ER26SGIF}
permits refer to IFN-γ bTB positive animals. As mentioned above, these
did not exist on AHCS prior to May 2019.

\section{Questions}\label{questions}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Why is there a peak in lesions in bTB positive animals at the start of
  the year (seems more pronounced in SICTT?)? Is this simply an
  administrative issue whereby there is a delay in sending animals to a
  factory?
\item
  Would it have anything to do with a slight increase in avian readings
  around Sept (this is seen in IFN-γ, we presume the same in SICTT)? The
  animals have been exposed to the environment for longer at this stage?
\item
  Avian is lower at the start of the year - is this because the majority
  of animals will be immunosurpressed (pregnant)(IFN-γ is mainly testing
  dairy animals)
\end{itemize}

\chapter{\texorpdfstring{Prevalence of confirmed \emph{M.bovis} lesions
found in animals at routine
slaughter}{Prevalence of confirmed M.bovis lesions found in animals at routine slaughter}}\label{sec-healthlylesion}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\(~\) \(~\)

\newpage

\section{Introduction}\label{introduction-1}

This document presents the prevalence of animals with confirmed
\emph{m.bovis} lesions found at routine slaughter (also known as
`factory lesion cases').

\textbf{NOTE: This is a draft, live document - it will be updated
periodically as we obtain more data. The most current date may vary
between output as we do not receive all the relevant data at once e.g.,
we obtain data for the previous year from the AIM system in the later
half of the current year and as a result we won't be able to estimate
the lesion rate within healthly slaughtered animals. This also means
that we don't have last years herd-type or herd-size AIM related
metrics. In the case of herd-type, we carry the latest estimate forward
until the new data comes in.}

\section{Definitions}\label{definitions-1}

\subsection{Proportion of animals with lesions at routine
slaughter}\label{proportion-of-animals-with-lesions-at-routine-slaughter-1}

Lab file:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# $description}
\CommentTok{\#                       .     n       percent}
\CommentTok{\#            INCONCLUSIVE  3715 0.03394554094}
\CommentTok{\#           OTHER REACTOR   873 0.00797697368}
\CommentTok{\#                RESEARCH     2 0.00001827485}
\CommentTok{\#               SINGLETON  7463 0.06819261696}
\CommentTok{\#         SLAUGHTER CHECK 87406 0.79866593567}
\CommentTok{\#  SLAUGHTER CHECK (ER36)  1246 0.01138523392}
\CommentTok{\#   SPECIAL INVESTIGATION  7039 0.06431834795}
\CommentTok{\#             STRAIN TYPE  1696 0.01549707602}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

AIM database:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\#   data\_type no\_by\_type}
\CommentTok{\#   \textless{}chr\textgreater{}          \textless{}int\textgreater{}}
\CommentTok{\# 1 ABAT         1695012}
\CommentTok{\# 2 DoF          4992685}
\CommentTok{\# 3 EXP          4467609}
\CommentTok{\# 4 F\_to\_F      20715340}
\CommentTok{\# 5 FACT        31456498}
\CommentTok{\# 6 IMP           237290}
\CommentTok{\# 7 Mart        29832107}
\CommentTok{\# 8 OLD          1218543}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\textbf{Numerator}:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Extracted \texttt{SLAUGHTER\ CHECK} and
  \texttt{SLAUGHTER\ CHECK\ (ER36)} (negative animal came from a herd
  that is in a bTB breakdown)
\item
  dropped any that had a positive SICTT or positive IFN-γ result
\item
  positive lab result (even if \texttt{lesion\ =\ "NVL"}? must have been
  positive on \texttt{macroscopic}, \texttt{hist}, \texttt{culture} or
  \textbf{PCR}?)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Denominator}:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  From AIM database, extracted
  \texttt{data\_type\ \ \%in\%\ \ c("FACT",\ "ABAT")} (excluding
  \texttt{DoF} as it is not routine to do inspections in knackeries,
  which we think includes young reactor calves - are these killed on
  farm first?) - John - You are not legally allowed bring a live animal
  to a knackery
\item
  summarised by \texttt{move\_from\_final}
\item
  merged in herd-type from AHCS/Brock et al.\\
\item
  subtracted count of animals already classified as a bTB case via SICTT
  or IFN-γ
\end{itemize}

\subsection{Lesions}\label{lesions}

We classified lesions into head or chest:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{lesion\_types}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Some animals have \texttt{NVL} but a positive result in the lab file
even though they are \texttt{SLAUGHTER\ CHECK} or
\texttt{SLAUGHTER\ CHECK\ (ER36)}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\#        head\_or\_chest     n     percent valid\_percent}
\CommentTok{\#                Chest 12742 0.385863969    0.39113485}
\CommentTok{\#                 Head 10285 0.311459027    0.31571354}
\CommentTok{\#     Multiple lesions  6940 0.210162922    0.21303374}
\CommentTok{\#  Non{-}visible lesions  2464 0.074616922    0.07563619}
\CommentTok{\#                Other   146 0.004421295    0.00448169}
\CommentTok{\#                 \textless{}NA\textgreater{}   445 0.013475865            NA}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

In my overall lesion prevalence calculations, I have included these
\texttt{NVL} cases which we came to conclude is correct as they had an
overall lab positive result.

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

Interesting that beef (which are primarly suckler herds) seem to be
sending less animals to slaughter and are less erratic in terms of
sending pattern in a year (in recent years).

Possible reasons:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  The number of beef herds are going down in general
\item
  They are sending their animals to fatteners/traders
\end{itemize}

Dairy send most animals to slaughter around drying off period (60 days
prior to calving)

\subsection{Lesion prevalence}\label{lesion-prevalence-1}

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{lesion_prevalence_trends_files/figure-pdf/fig-ahcshealtylesion-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-ahcshealtylesion}Overall lesion prevalence at
routine slaughter by AHCS herd-type.}

\end{figure}%

Why is suckler/beef higher than dairy (if we think of dairy as being the
primary problem in terms of transmission e.g.~dairy intensification
etc)?

Possible explanations:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Possibly a consequence of testing
\item
  Dairy vs suckler/beef testing is completely different
\item
  Suckler herds are wilder and harder to test, dairy are usually much
  easier to test
\item
  Possibly more incentive in a large dairy herd to quickly get rid of
  the problem before it goes through the herd (sucklers will be more out
  in the open and possibly less chance of spreading quickly)
\item
  Infection is probably caught earlier in dairy herds (as they are
  easier to test and more IFN-γ testing is also done which should get
  earlier infection)
\end{itemize}

\(~\) \(~\)

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\(~\) \(~\)

\section{Notes}\label{notes}

Why would lesions in head/chest be increasing? - change in way recording
is done? - differences between factories? - A very small minority might
specialise in kosher meats and so may inspect animals differently but
unlike to have an effect nationally

We might expect dairy might have higher lesion rates than beef herds
(intensification hypothesis of dairy herds) - why dont we see this? -
possible age effects in dairy? Might need to stratify by age/sex

Replicate above for bTB cases

From DAFM:

What are the average lesion detection rates? - 30\% of skin test
reactors will have visible lesions at post-mortem inspection. - 15\% of
Gamma Interferon reactors will have visible lesions at post-mortem
inspection. (This figure relates to cattle, in herds with high risk
breakdowns, which are in the same groups as cattle that were skin test
reactors).

\textbf{Biology of lesion trends}

\begin{itemize}
\item
  Healthy lesion rate seems to mirror reactor lesion rate (previous
  chapter) which suggests, these are not historic infections but
  relatively new?
\item
  Why were levels high in 2008? It seems to be the peak of that cycle at
  the time and then there was a serious effort to clean-up the whole
  system (concerted effort): there were focused investigations,
  hot-spots were priortised (e.g., Monaghan), fraud cases were
  investigated, the quality control programme was brought in, SICTT \&
  PVP training was increased/introduced, there was much more awareness
  to look out for lesions due to the ongoing gamma trial at the time
  etc. IFN-γ rates were up around 30\% indicating that we were missing
  lots of infection at the time. This may also partly explain why chest
  prevalence was high back in 2008 - we were missing lots of infection
  and it had seeded in animals.
\item
  Difference in proportion of chest vs head lesions. Not seeing a
  difference in beef/sucklers but are in dairy - suggests this is real
  effect? The increase in chest compared to head in dairy is consistent
  with increased immunosuppression. bTB is a chest disease, if an animal
  is stressed it will stay in the lungs (before eventually spreading
  around the body when it has reached a more advanced stage). If an
  animal is immunosuppressed, they won't initially get multiple lesions
  - which seems counter intuitive. Their immune system is not competent
  enough to disseminate the disease around the body. More stress = more
  chest involvement. If you get more lung infection, you get more
  transmission.
\item
  Why is the ``multiple lesions'' group decreasing? This may be related
  to the improvement in detection since 2008 which may also mean earlier
  detection. Multiple lesions are a reflection of more advanced
  infection but we are generally detecting bTB at an early stage now
  with tighter testing quality (QA programme and IFN-γ etc.).
\item
  What is ideal weather for mbovis? - probably warm, moist soil. Mild,
  damp rather than dry.
\item
  Re: the lesions, they could take six months to develop, so the later
  summer lesion profile could well reflect infection dynamics the
  previous March
\end{itemize}

Policy:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  I wonder would it be worth considering additionally testing for herds
  with a lot of chest detections (round of IFN-γ testing)? Or even herds
  with animals with multiple lesions (more advanced infection)? I am
  checking the data on this
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Questions/ideas to explore}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Whats interesting is that dairy seem to send animals primarily to
  slaughter at the start of the year (presumably during drying period?)
  but have the most lesions presented at slaughter in the 2nd half of
  the year. Perhaps these lesioned animals come from a subset of dairy
  herds who are not spring calving herds?
\item
  why do we see a clean, consistent seasonal effect of an increase in
  lesion prevalence later in the year?
\item
  Does it suggests that there a cohort of riskier herds, that send their
  animals to slaughter later in the year (and perhaps, do not send
  animals earlier in the year and so we miss their infections)- I need
  to check this.
\item
  Change in sensitivity by time of year? Or is it related to previous
  point, that riskier herds are just not sending their animals at that
  time? Or, they are not riskier per se, but as they don't have a round
  test earlier in the year, earlier infections are not detected after
  winter housing and infection is allowed to seed in the animals and
  lesions form.
\item
  Is there a difference in BD size between a herd with more chest vs
  head vs multiple lesions? Hypothesis here is that herds with more
  multiple lesions/chest, may have more advanced disease and increased
  likelihood of having more/missed infection. I need to check this.
\item
  It would be really interesting to get the age profile of these -- I
  will do these at some stage for my age work anyway but it will just
  take a bit of work. What we are seeing could be a certain aged animal
  that tends to get sent to slaughter later in the year.
\item
  Is there a reporting bias? Does inspection start at head?
\item
  Redo to include Brock sub-classes: may help tease out which herds are
  sending animals at later time of year
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Follow-up on lesion detection/inspection biases}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Usually two VIs in the factory who act in a supervisory role.
  Temporary VI (TVIs) do the inspections and there are usually 4 TVIs
  working at one time. TVIs are usually private vets who do this as
  additional work. You have to be a vet to do meat inspection in Ireland
  (although this might not be the rule in other countries?)
\item
  Process of inspection
\item
  The crucial piece of information is that (at least in this factory but
  should be similar in others) different body parts go to different
  people for inspection so one TVI will not inspect the whole body of
  one animal.
\item
  Each animal is distributed into 4 separate process lines:

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    Head
  \item
    Pluck (lung, chest)
  \item
    Belly (intestines etc?)
  \item
    inspection of the split carcass (carcass hoisted up)
  \end{itemize}
\item
  TVIs will be stationed at one of these lines for the duration of their
  shifts but will probably rotate lines between shifts (e.g.~so one TVI
  would not always be stationed on the head line)
\item
  In this particular factory head and pluck lines were close together so
  the TVIs could theoretically talk to each other but more than likely
  that the inspection is independent
\item
  Multiple lesions (MLs): need to clarify if this means multiple lesions
  within the one station (so a TVI spots multiple lesions in the head
  line) or does it mean lesions were spotted in multiple body part
  locations. Wasn't sure if ML is assigned by TVI on their line of at
  the end of all the stations. I suppose it could be a mixture of both?
\item
  In general, TVIs have moved away from diagnosing a lesion as mbovis
  (in healthy animals), any suspect lesion is generally just sent to the
  laboratory for testing
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Reactor inspections (animals classified as bTB before entering
factory via SICTT or GIF)}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  In this factory, reactors were inspected at a different time to the
  healthy animals (e.g.~altogether after lunch). TVIs were told that
  these were reactor animals. The lines were slowed down a little bit
  (compared to healthy animal) which naturally gave them a small bit
  more of a thorough inspection. In terms of detecting lesions,
  naturally, there may be some confirmation bias at play here already
  knowing that they were reactor animals?
\end{itemize}

\section{Brock et al herd-types
definitions}\label{brock-et-al-herd-types-definitions}

\textbf{Dairy}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Residency in the herd is longer than 30 days for at least 50\% of the
  herd.
\item
  Proportion of animals in a herd that have ever calved by September,
  relative to herd size is greater than 25\%.
\item
  66\% or more of the animals are of a dairy breed.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Beef}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  (generally just suckler from AHCS I think)
\item
  Residency in the herd is longer than 30 days for at least 50\% of the
  herd
\item
  Proportion of animals in a herd that have ever calved by September,
  relative to herd size is greater than 25\%.
\item
  Less than 33\% of the animals are of a dairy breed.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Mixed}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Residency in the herd is longer than 30 days for at least 50\% of the
  herd.
\item
  Proportion of animals in a herd that have ever calved by September,
  relative to herd size is greater than 25\%.
\item
  33\% or more of the animals and less than 66\% are of a dairy breed.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Store}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Residency in the herd is longer than 30 days for at least 50\% of the
  herd.
\item
  Less than 25\% of the animals in the herd have calved in the past
\item
  Less than 50\% of the animals in the herd go directly to slaughter
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Fattener}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Residency in the herd is longer than 30 days for at least 50\% of the
  herd.
\item
  Proportion of animals in a herd that have ever calved by September,
  relative to herd size is greater than 25\%.
\item
  50\% or more of the animals in the herd go directly to slaughter.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Trader}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  50\% or more of the animals spend less than 30 days in the herd.
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Seasonal}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Animals are kept in the herd for only part of the year. (As of 2024,
  ``seasonal'' is no longer a category - they have been moved into
  ``trader'' and ``fattener'' categories respectively)
\end{itemize}

\textbf{Unknown} - Self-explanatory.

\part{Breakdowns (BDs)}

\chapter{Exploration of herd-level bTB breakdowns
(BDs)}\label{sec-breakdown}

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\(~\) \(~\)

\section{Breakdown (BD) definition}\label{breakdown-bd-definition}

For this study, we defined the start date of a BD in a herd as the date
when an animal was disclosed as a bTB case, as determined by the SICTT,
IFN-γ or by the detection of visible lesions at routine slaughter which
are then laboratory confirmed with \emph{M. bovis} infection (also known
as `factory lesion cases'). In the situation when a BD started due to
the detection of a visible lesion, we chose the start date as the date
when the sample was sent to the lab and not the date in which it was
identified as culture positive which could be a number of weeks later.
Depending on the circumstances, the herds \texttt{trading\_status} on
AHCS will generally change from \texttt{free} to
\texttt{withdrawn}/\texttt{suspended}. We defined the BD end date as the
date of the next SICTT (whole herd) test where the
\texttt{trading\_status} was restored to \texttt{free}. Once a bTB case
is detected, a herd will usually remain in a BD until it has had two
consecutive, clear whole-herd SICTT tests (usually 60 days apart). There
are certain exceptions to the above criteria e.g., singletons.

There were rare instances when a herd was in a BD based on trading
status but it did not have a bTB case (these could be for awhole host of
reasons including fraud investigations (so a herd may be in
\texttt{suspended} mode although no cases), error on system etc.). Of
all BDs after 2007 (n = 81168), there were only 601 of these zero case
BDs (0.74\%). The initiating/index SICTT of these BDs is given below:

\(~\) \(~\)

\begin{verbatim}
 bd_first_skin_test_type   n     percent
                       1  55 0.091514143
                     10A  16 0.026622296
                       2   2 0.003327787
                       4  24 0.039933444
                      5A  18 0.029950083
                      5B  14 0.023294509
                      5C   8 0.013311148
                      5E   5 0.008319468
                      5F  65 0.108153078
                      5G   5 0.008319468

\section{BD size}\label{bd-size}

Here, we explore the number of bTB cases disclosed within herd BDs
(i.e.~BD size). This illustrates that BD size (e.g., median) is
increasing particulaly within dairy herds. The variance in the number of
cases within BDs is also increasing within dairy. Mixed herd-types are
also increasing in size (these are herds that have a component of their
herd as dairy). The fact that dairy herds have more bTB cases during a
BD is not that surprising as dairy are also the herds with the largest
number of animals (herd size **add link here) in their herd.

\(~\) \(~\)

\subsection{STL decomposition}\label{stl-decomposition}

As an initial, exploratory step, we applied a seasonal-trend
decomposition procedure based on loess (locally estimated scatterplot
smoothing, a type of locally-weighted polynomial regression),
abbreviated to STL {[}9{]}. The purpose of the decomposition of a time
series is to ``deconstruct'' it into a number of component series which,
when combined, will reproduce the original time series. Using STL, the
specific aim is to split a seasonal time series into three additive
components: the underlying trend, seasonality and the remainder (noise).

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{time_series_files/figure-pdf/fig-stlplot-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-stlplot}STL decomposition of bTB cases by month.}

\end{figure}%

\begin{center}\rule{0.5\linewidth}{0.5pt}\end{center}

\(~\) \(~\)

\subsection{Herd vs animal level
test-types}\label{herd-vs-animal-level-test-types}

full herd test (e.g., a pre-movement test). We assigned each test-type
to a herd or animal level test:
